Circles in diffraction
Emily R. Korfanty, Nicolae Strungaru

Abstract
. Given a Fourier transformable measure in two dimensions, we find a formula for the intensity of its Fourier transform along circles. In particular, we obtain a formula for the diffraction measure along a circle in terms of the autocorrelation measure. We look at some applications of this formula.



1.
Introduction

Since the early 1900s, crystallographers have used diffraction experiments to determine the structure of crystals. Until the discovery of quasicrystals
[
SBGC84
]
by Dan Shechtman in 1984, pure point diffraction was taken as evidence of an underlying periodic structure in the material. Quasicrystals, however, exhibit sharp diffraction patterns, and typically show symmetries that are impossible in materials with a periodic structure. Shechtman was later awarded the 2011 Nobel Prize in Chemistry for the discovery, which also inspired a new interest in aperiodic structures and mathematical diffraction theory. For an overview of this area, we recommend the monographs
[
BG13
,
BG17
]
.

The rigorous mathematical treatment of diffraction was introduced by Hof in
[
Hof95
]
. He defined the diffraction measure as the Fourier transform of the so-called
autocorrelation measure
of the underlying structure.
Diffraction is now well understood for systems with pure point spectrum
[
LSS20
,
LSS24
]
, and systems coming from cut-and-project schemes
[
Sch00
,
BM04
,
RS17
,
Str05
,
Str17
,
Str20
,
BHS17
,
KR16
]
.

On the other hand, the diffraction for systems without finite local complexity (FLC) is, in general, much less understood. One such example, the pinwheel tiling
[
Rad94
]
, is produced by a simple 2-dimensional substitution. The tiles in the pinwheel tiling appear in infinitely many orientations, and therefore the pinwheel does not have FLC. Its diffraction is rotationally invariant
[
MPS06
]
, and has a Bragg peak of unit intensity at the origin, but nothing else is known about its diffraction. In particular, it is not known if the diffraction is absolutely continuous, singular continuous or mixed.

There are many examples of rotational invariant diffraction patterns. For example, any substitution with
statistical circular symmetry
, meaning that the orientations of the tiles distribute uniformly around the unit circle (
[
Fre08
]

[
Sad98
]
,
[
FHG24
]
), leading to a rotational invariant diffraction pattern. More generally, the diffraction of any rotational invariant measure is also rotational invariant.

Rotational invariance also appears in powder diffraction, which is a slightly different diffraction technique, where the sample is ground into many smaller samples prior to the usual diffraction process. During the grinding process, the samples tend to rotate in random directions. This random distribution of rotations on the unit circle is typically statistically circular, and hence the powder diffraction is rotational invariant.

In
[
BFG07
,
BFG07a
,
GD11
]
, numerical approximations of the pinwheel diffraction were compared to a powder diffraction from
{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}
. These numerical approximations suggest that the pinwheel diffraction may include bright circles with the same radii as the circles in the powder diffraction from
{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}
, which would imply the existence of a singular continuous component of the spectrum. Despite this observed similarity to the powder diffraction, it is still unknown whether or not there exists any circle in the pinwheel diffraction. Besides numerical simulations, which are not too precise for this particular system, we are not aware of any progress made toward understanding the diffraction of the pinwheel tiling in the last 20 years.

Motivated by the above, in this paper we develop a method for computing the intensity of a circle in the diffraction. This may provide a new tool to study the diffraction of substitution tilings with statistical circular symmetry, such as the pinwheel and its related families
[
Sad98
,
Fre08
,
FHG24
]
. In Section
5
, we derive the following explicit limit formula for the diffraction intensity
\hbox{\hskip 451.29065pt\hskip-2.58865pt\hbox{$\gamma$}\hskip-2.58865pt\hskip-451.29065pt\raisebox{15.30566pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-3.34055pt}{\resizebox{53617400}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14236pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.98pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14285pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.5pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}\hskip-451.29065pt\hskip along the circle
C_{r}=\{x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}:\|x\|=r\}
in terms of the autocorrelation measure
\gamma
:

Theorem
5.4
.

Let
\gamma
be an autocorrelation measure on
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
and let
r>0
. Then

\hbox{\hskip 441.65944pt\hskip-2.58865pt\hbox{$\gamma$}\hskip-2.58865pt\hskip-441.65944pt\raisebox{15.30566pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-3.34055pt}{\resizebox{52312104}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14236pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.98pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14285pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt} 441.65944pt}(C_{r})=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{2r\pi\sqrt{\pi}}{n}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}e^{-\pi^{2}\|y\|^{2}/n^{2}}J_{0}(2\pi r\|y\|)\,\mbox{d}\gamma(y)\,.

Here
J_{0}
represents the Bessel function of the first kind (see Definition
3.1
).

1_{C_{r}}=0
almost everywhere with respect to the Lebesgue measure on

, and hence

451.29065pt}=0
. To get around this issue, we instead approximate
1_{C_{r}}
by a sequence of Schwartz functions of the form

f_{n}:=g_{n}*\theta_{r}\,,

where
\theta_{r}
is the uniform probability measure on
C_{r}
(see Definition
3.1
), and
g_{n}
is an approximate identity sequence of Gaussian functions, renormalized such that
f_{n}
converges to
1
on
C_{r}
. Using the formula
\hbox{\hskip 451.29065pt\hskip-2.58865pt\hbox{$\gamma$}\hskip-2.58865pt\hskip-451.29065pt\raisebox{15.30566pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-3.34055pt}{\resizebox{53617400}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14236pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.98pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14285pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt} 451.29065pt})
and letting
n\to\infty
we obtain Theorem
5.4
. The Bessel function
J_{0}
appears naturally in this approach through the Fourier transform of
\theta_{r}
; see Proposition
3.4
.

Our paper is organized as follows. We start by reviewing the basic definitions and framework in Section
2.2
. We use the Fourier transform of strongly tempered measures (see Definition
2.5
), which extends the classical theory of Fourier transformable measures in
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
. In Section
3
we briefly review the Bessel functions
J_{0}
and
I_{0}
and some of their properties. As emphasized above,
J_{0}
appears as the Fourier transform of
\theta_{r}
, while
I_{0}
appears naturally in the convolution of
\theta_{r}
with the Gaussian function(s).

Section
4
provides the fundamental tool for proving Theorem
5.4
. We show, via a straightforward application of the dominated convergence theorem, that the intensity

, whenever when the sequence

451.29065pt}(y)\,\mbox{d}\gamma(y)
, we obtain a more general version of Proposition

(see Corollary
4.4
).
Theorem
5.4
is then obtained by constructing in Section
5
an explicit approximate
r
-circle with easily calculable Fourier transform.

In Section
6
, we complete the paper by looking at several examples and applications of Theorem
5.4
. In particular, the Poisson summation formula in
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
yields the following result for the sum of two squares function:

Proposition
6.13
.

The sum of two squares function
r_{2}(n)
satisfies the following:

(a)

For all
k\in\mathbb{N}
we have

r_{2}(k)=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{2\pi\sqrt{\pi k}}{n}\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}r_{2}(m)e^{-\frac{\pi^{2}m}{n^{2}}}J_{0}(2\pi\sqrt{km})\,.

(b)

For all
r>0
such that
r^{2}\notin\mathbb{N}
we have

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{2r\pi\sqrt{\pi}}{n}\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}r_{2}(m)e^{-\frac{\pi^{2}m\textbf{}}{n^{2}}}J_{0}(2\pi r\sqrt{m})=0\,.

In Appendix
A
, we construct a different family of approximate
r
-circles via convolutions with annuli of small width.

2.
Preliminaries

2.1.
Definitions and notations

Throughout this paper,
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
denotes the Euclidean space of dimension
d\geq 1
. We will denote the standard Euclidean norm on
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
by
\|\cdot\|
. We will primarily be interested in the case
d=2
.

Given
R>0
, we denote by
B_{R}
the open ball

B_{R}:=\{x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\,:\,\|x\|<R\}\,,

of radius
R
, and by
\overline{B_{R}}
the closed ball

\overline{B_{R}}:=\{x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\,:\,\|x\|\leq R\}\,.

We will use
1_{A}
to denote the characteristic function of a set
A\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
, i.e.

1_{A}(x):=\begin{cases}1&x\in A\\
0&x\notin A\,.\end{cases}

As usual,
C_{\mathsf{c}}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
denotes the space of continuous functions with compact support, and
C_{\mathsf{c}}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
is the subspace of
C_{\mathsf{c}}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
consisting of smooth (infinitely many times differentiable) functions. The space of Schwartz functions is denoted by
{\mathcal{S}}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
and the space of tempered distributions is denoted by
{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
.

If
f
is any bounded function, we will use the notation

\|f\|_{\infty}:=\sup_{x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}|f(x)|\,,

for the supremum norm of
f
.

Let us recall that the
Fourier transform
of a function
f\in L^{1}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
is the function
\hbox{\hskip 451.29065pt\hskip-2.98613pt\hbox{$f$}\hskip-2.98613pt\hskip-451.29065pt\raisebox{17.94456pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-3.34055pt}{\resizebox{53617400}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14236pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.98pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14285pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.5pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}\hskip-451.29065pt\hskip 451.29065pt}:{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\to{\mathbb{C}}
defined by

\hbox{\hskip , via

For a tempered distribution
T\in{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
we will refer to the tempered distribution

T

\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt

as the
distributional Fourier transform of
T
.

2.2.
Strongly tempered measures

In this paper, we prove our results for a space of measures with a certain property (see Definition
2.5
below) that is compatible with diffraction theory. Let us first recall the definition of a measure.

Definition 2.1
.

A
Radon measure
on
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
is a linear functional
\mu:C_{\mathsf{c}}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})\rightarrow{\mathbb{C}}
with the property that for every compact set
K\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
, there exists a constant
a_{K}
such that

(2.2)

|\mu(f)|\leq a_{K}\|f\|_{\infty}\,,

holds for every
f\in C_{\mathsf{c}}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
with
\mbox{\rm supp}(f)\subseteq K
. We write
{\mathcal{M}}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
for the space of all Radon measures on
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
. We will simply refer to Radon measures as measures.

We will often use the so-called
variation measure

|\mu|
, where
\mu
is some measure. The definition of
|\mu|
is provided in the following theorem:

Theorem 2.2

(
[
RS17
, Lemma C.2]
,
[
Ped95
,
\nopp
6.5.8]
)
.

For every measure
\mu
, there exists a smallest positive measure
|\mu|
satisfying

|\mu(f)|\leq|\mu|(|f|)\qquad\forall f\in C_{\mathsf{c}}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})\,.

Moreover,
|\mu|
is the unique measure with the property that

|\mu|(f)=\sup\{|\mu(g)|:g\in C_{\mathsf{c}}({\mathbb{R}}^{d}),|g|\leq f\}\,,

holds for all non-negative functions
f\in C_{\mathsf{c}}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
.
∎

Below, we will be using the Fourier transform in the distributional sense for measures. Let us first note that, in general,
\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}f(x)\,\mbox{d}\mu(x)
may not make sense for a measure
\mu
and Schwartz function
f
. Since we will work with integrals of this type, we will need to restrict the class of measures we are studying. We start by recalling the following definition from
[
BS23
]
:

Definition 2.3
.

A measure
\mu
is called
1
1

1

In
[
AG74
]
the authors call such a measure slowly increasing. The term strongly tempered is used in
[
BS23
]
.

strongly tempered
if there exists a polynomial
P\in{\mathbb{C}}[x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{d}]
such that

\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\frac{1}{1+|P(x)|}\,\mbox{d}|\mu|(x)<\infty\,.

The following fact about strongly tempered measures explains why this definition is important for us.

Fact 2.4

(
[
BS23
, Theorem 2.7]
)
.

A measure
\mu
is strongly tempered if and only if we have
\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}|f(t)|\mbox{d}|\mu|(t)<\infty
for all
f\in{\mathcal{S}}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
. Moreover, in this case, the map
T_{\mu}:{\mathcal{S}}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})\rightarrow{\mathbb{C}}
given by

T_{\mu}(f):=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}f(t)\mbox{d}\mu(t)\,,

defines a tempered distribution. Furthermore, in this case, the measure
\mu
and the tempered distribution
T_{\mu}
uniquely determine each other.

Let us now discuss the Fourier transformability of strongly tempered measures in the distributional sense. Every strongly tempered measure has a distributional Fourier transform; however, this Fourier transform is a tempered distribution that is not necessarily a measure. Even if it is a measure, it may not be strongly tempered (see for example
[
AG74
, Prop. 7.1]
or
[
BS23
, Sect. 3]
). Since our results rely on the distributional Fourier transform being a strongly tempered measure, we introduce the following definition.

Definition 2.5
.

A measure
\mu
is called
Fourier transformable in the strongly tempered sense
if
\mu
is a strongly tempered measure and there exists a strongly tempered measure
\nu
such that, as tempered distributions, we have

\hbox{\hskip 451.29065pt\hskip-6.29749pt\hbox{$T_{\mu}$}\hskip-6.29749pt\hskip-451.29065pt\raisebox{17.83344pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-3.34055pt}{\resizebox{53617400}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14236pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.98pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14285pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.5pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}\hskip-451.29065pt\hskip 451.29065pt}=T_{\nu}\,.

If this is the case, we write
\hbox{\hskip 451.29065pt\hskip-3.01274pt\hbox{$\mu$}\hskip-3.01274pt\hskip-451.29065pt\raisebox{15.30566pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-3.34055pt}{\resizebox{53617400}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14236pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.98pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14285pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\r 451.29065pt}:=\nu
and call it the
Fourier transform of
\mu
in the strongly tempered sense
.

We write
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{M}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
for the space of all strongly tempered measures
\mu
which are Fourier transformable in the strongly tempered sense.

In the rest of the paper, all measures are assumed to be strongly tempered. Whenever we say that a measure
\mu
is Fourier transformable, it is understood to mean Fourier transformable in the strongly tempered sense. Let us make some important remarks.

Remark 2.6
.

(a)

Let
\mu
be a strongly tempered measure. Then
\mu
is Fourier transformable in the measure sense (see
[
AG74
,
MS17
]
for a definition) if and only if
\mu\in\mathcal{S}\mathcal{M}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
and

\mu

\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt

is translation bounded
[
SS21
]
. Moreover, in this case the two Fourier transforms coincide.

In particular, if
\mu
is a strongly tempered measure that is also Fourier transformable in the measure sense, then
\mu\in\mathcal{S}\mathcal{M}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
and the Fourier transforms coincide. This allows us to use the same notation for both Fourier transforms.

(b)

Typically, the diffraction measure

\gamma

\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt

is defined as the Fourier transform in the measure sense of the autocorrelation measure
\gamma
. In general,
\gamma
is a strongly tempered measure. Therefore, by (a), the diffraction measure

\gamma

\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt

is the Fourier transform in the strongly tempered sense of
\gamma
.

We now list some easy observations about
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{M}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
:

Fact 2.7
.

Let
\mu\in\mathcal{S}\mathcal{M}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
. Then the following statements hold:

(a)

The measures
\mu
and

\mu

\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt

uniquely determine each other.

(b)

For all
f\in{\mathcal{S}}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
we have

441.65944pt}$}\hskip-441.65944pt\hskip-441.65944pt\raisebox{17.44456pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-3.34055pt}{\resizebox{52312104}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14236pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.98pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14285pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.5pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}\hskip-441.65944pt\hskip 441.65944pt}=\mu^{\dagger}\,,

where
\mu^{\dagger}
is the measure defined by
\mu^{\dagger}(f):=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}f(-x)\,\mbox{d}\mu(x)
.

In particular, the Fourier transform is a bijection from
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{M}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
onto
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{M}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
.

The results in this paper rely on these observations.

Let us conclude this section by recalling a classical result about convolutions that we will need in Section
5
.

Theorem 2.8

(
[
Gra08
, Theorem 2.3.20]
)
.

Let
T\in{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
be a tempered distribution, and let
f\in{\mathcal{S}}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
be a Schwartz function. Then
f*T
is a
C^{\infty}
function, where

f*T(\varphi):=T(f^{\dagger}*\varphi)\qquad\forall\varphi\in{\mathcal{S}}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})\,,

and
f^{\dagger}
denotes the function given by
f^{\dagger}(x)=g(-x)
for all
x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
.
Furthermore, if
T
has compact support, then
f*T
is a Schwartz function.∎

Remark 2.9
.

Any measure of compact support is a strongly tempered measure. Moreover,
a strongly tempered measure
\mu
has compact support if and only if the tempered distribution
T_{\mu}
is compactly supported as a distribution.

3.
Bessel functions

In Section
5
we are going to run into certain integrals. It turns out that these are exactly the integrals defining the so called Bessel function
J_{0}
of first kind and order zero, and the modified Bessel function
I_{0}
of the first kind and order zero. The asymptotic behaviour of
I_{0}
also plays an important role in our computations. In this section we collect the results about these functions that will be important for us.

3.1.
J_{0}
and the Fourier transform of circles

Definition 3.1
.

For each
t\geq 0
, define

J_{0}(t):=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}e^{-it\cos\theta}\,\mbox{d}\theta\,.

J_{0}
is called the
Bessel function of the first kind and order 0
.

Since
\cos\theta
is
2\pi
-periodic, it is immediate that for all
a\in{\mathbb{R}}
we have

\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{a}^{a+\pi}e^{-it\cos\theta}\,\mbox{d}\theta=J_{0}(t)\,.

The Bessel function
J_{0}
will be important for the following reason: the Fourier transform of the uniform probability measure of a circle is given by a
J_{0}
function. Let us make this statement precise.

Definition 3.2
.

For
r>0
, define
\theta_{r}:C_{\mathsf{c}}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})\rightarrow{\mathbb{C}}
by

\theta_{r}(f)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}f(r\cos\theta,r\sin\theta)\,\mbox{d}\theta\qquad\forall f\in C_{\mathsf{c}}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})\,.

Then,
\theta_{r}
is a probability measure with compact support
C_{r}:=\{x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}:\|x\|=r\}
.

Remark 3.3
.

Note that
C_{r}
is a compact Abelian group under the group operation

(re^{i\theta})\diamond(re^{i\varphi}):=re^{i(\theta+\varphi)}\,.

Then,
\theta_{r}
can be seen as the unique probability Haar measure on
(C_{r},\diamond)
, embedded into
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
.

Since
\theta_{r}
has compact support, it defines a strongly tempered measure, and we can easily compute its Fourier transform using (
2.1
).

Proposition 3.4
.

Let
r>0
. The Fourier transform of
\theta_{r}
is an absolutely continuous measure with density function

\hbox{\hskip 441.65944pt\hskip-4.56134pt\hbox{$\theta_{r}$}\hskip-4.56134pt\hskip-441.65944pt\raisebox{17.94456pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-3.34055pt}{\resizebox{52312104}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14236pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.98pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14285pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.5pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}\hskip-441.65944pt\hskip 441.65944pt}(y)=J_{0}(2\pi r\|y\|)\qquad\forall y\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\,.

Proof.

Since
\theta_{r}
is a distribution with compact support, its Fourier transform is an absolutely continuous measure with density function given by

\displaystyle\hbox{\hskip 451.29065pt\hskip-4.56134pt\hbox{$\theta_{r}$}\hskip-4.56134pt\hskip-451.29065pt\raisebox{17.94456pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-3.34055pt}{\resizebox{53617400}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14236pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.98pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14285pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.5pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}\hskip-451.29065pt\hskip 451.29065pt}(y)

\displaystyle=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}e^{-2\pi ix\cdot y}\,\mbox{d}\theta_{r}(x)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}e^{-2\pi ir(y_{1}\cos\theta+y_{2}\sin\theta)}\mbox{d}\theta\qquad\forall y=(y_{1},y_{2})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\,.

Consider
y=(R\cos\phi,R\sin\phi)
for some
R\geq 0
and
\phi\in[-\pi,\pi)
. We have

\displaystyle\hbox{\hskip 451.29065pt\hskip-4.56134pt\hbox{$\theta_{r}$}\hskip-4.56134pt\hskip-451.29065pt\raisebox{17.94456pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-3.34055pt}{\resizebox{53617400}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14236pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.98pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14285pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.5pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}\hskip-451.29065pt\hskip 451.29065pt}(R\cos(\phi),R\sin(\phi))

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}e^{-2\pi irR\cos(\phi+\theta)}\mbox{d}\theta

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\psi=\phi+\theta}}{{=\joinrel=\joinrel=\joinrel=\joinrel=}}\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\phi-\pi}^{\phi+\pi}e^{-2\pi irR\cos\psi}\mbox{d}\psi=J_{0}(2\pi rR)\,.

This shows that
\hbox{\hskip 451.29065pt\hskip-4.56134pt\hbox{$\theta_{r}$}\hskip-4.56134pt\hskip-451.29065pt\raisebox{17.94456pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-3.34055pt}{\resizebox{53617400}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14236pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.98pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14285pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.5pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}\hskip-451.29065pt\hskip 451.29065pt}(y)=J_{0}(2\pi r\|y\|)
for all
y\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
, as desired.
∎

3.2.
I_{0}
and its asymptotic behaviour

Definition 3.5
.

For each
t\geq 0
, define

I_{0}(t):=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}e^{t\cos\theta}\,\mbox{d}\theta\,.

I_{0}
is called the
modified Bessel function of the first kind of order 0
.

The function
I_{0}
will appear naturally in Section
5
as the convolution of a Gaussian function and
\theta_{r}
. This type of convolution will be essential to our approach. The following asymptotic property of
I_{0}
will be crucial in our computations:

Theorem 3.6

(Asymptotic behavior of
I_{0}
)
.

\lim_{t\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\sqrt{2\pi t}I_{0}(t)}{e^{t}}=1\,.

This result is well-known and follows from a standard application of Laplace’s method for asymptotic expansions of functions of the form

I(x)=\int_{a}^{b}e^{xg(t)}f(t)\,\mbox{d}t\,,

(refer to
[
BO99
, Ch. 6.4]
for the general theory of Laplace’s method).
The limit can easily be deduced from this general theory;
anyhow, for completeness, we include a short direct proof of Theorem
3.6
below.

First, we need the following lemma.

Lemma 3.7
.

For each
t\geq 0
, we have

I_{0}(t)=\frac{e^{t}}{\pi}\int_{0}^{2}\frac{e^{-t\xi}}{\sqrt{\xi(2-\xi)}}\,\mbox{d}\xi\,.

Proof.

Using symmetry and applying the substitution
\xi=1-\cos\theta
, we have

\displaystyle I_{0}(t)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}e^{t\cos\theta}\,\mbox{d}\theta=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\pi}e^{t\cos\theta}\,\mbox{d}\theta\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\xi=1-\cos\theta}}{{=\joinrel=\joinrel=\joinrel=\joinrel=\joinrel=\joinrel=}}\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{0}^{2}\frac{e^{t(1-\xi)}}{\sqrt{\xi(2-\xi)}}\,\mbox{d}\xi

\displaystyle=\frac{e^{t}}{\pi}\int_{0}^{2}\frac{e^{-t\xi}}{\sqrt{\xi(2-\xi)}}\,\mbox{d}\xi\,.

∎

We can now prove the asymptotic result.

Proof of Theorem
3.6
.

By Lemma
3.7
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{\pi I_{0}(t)}{e^{t}}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{2}\frac{e^{-t\xi}}{\sqrt{\xi(2-\xi)}}\,\mbox{d}\xi

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\int_{0}^{2}\frac{e^{-t\xi}}{\sqrt{2\xi}}\,\mbox{d}\xi}_{I_{1}}+\underbrace{\int_{0}^{1}\frac{e^{-t\xi}}{\sqrt{\xi}}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2-\xi}}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\right)\,\mbox{d}\xi}_{I_{2}}+\underbrace{\int_{1}^{2}\frac{e^{-t\xi}}{\sqrt{\xi}}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2-\xi}}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\right)\,\mbox{d}\xi}_{I_{3}}\,.

We now look at each of the three integrals one by one. First, with a simple substitution we get

\displaystyle I_{1}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle u=\sqrt{t\xi}}}{{=\joinrel=\joinrel=\joinrel=\joinrel=}}\sqrt{\frac{2}{t}}\int_{0}^{\sqrt{2t}}e^{-u^{2}}\mbox{d}u\,.

Then, using
\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}e^{-u^{2}}\,\mbox{d}u=\sqrt{\pi}
, we get

\displaystyle\left|\sqrt{\frac{2t}{\pi}}I_{1}-1\right|=\left|\frac{2}{\sqrt{\pi}}\int_{0}^{\sqrt{2t}}e^{-u^{2}}\mbox{d}u-1\right|

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{\sqrt{\pi}}\int_{\sqrt{2t}}^{\infty}e^{-u^{2}}\mbox{d}u

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{\sqrt{\pi}}\int_{\sqrt{2t}}^{\infty}e^{-u\sqrt{2t}}\mbox{d}u=\frac{2}{\sqrt{\pi}}\frac{e^{-2t}}{\sqrt{2t}}\,.

Next, observe that the function
f:[0,1]\to{\mathbb{R}}

f(\xi):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\xi}}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2-\xi}}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\right)&\mbox{ if }\xi\neq 0\\
0&\mbox{ if }\xi=0\,,\end{array}\right.

is continuous and hence bounded on
[0,1]
. Therefore, there exists some
M>0
such that

\displaystyle|I_{2}|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{1}Me^{-t\xi}\,\mbox{d}\xi=\frac{M}{t}(1-e^{-t})\,.

For the third integral, we use that
e^{-t\xi}\leq e^{-t}
for
t\geq 1
to obtain

\displaystyle|I_{3}|

\displaystyle\leq e^{-t}\int_{1}^{2}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\xi}}\left|\frac{1}{\sqrt{2-\xi}}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\right|\,\mbox{d}\xi=Ce^{-t}\,,

where

C:=\int_{1}^{2}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\xi}}\left|\frac{1}{\sqrt{2-\xi}}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\right|\,\mbox{d}\xi<\infty\,.

Putting everything together, we get

\displaystyle\left|\frac{\sqrt{2\pi t}I_{0}(t)}{e^{t}}-1\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\sqrt{\frac{2t}{\pi}}(I_{1}+I_{2}+I_{3})-1\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left|\sqrt{\frac{2t}{\pi}}I_{1}-1\right|+\sqrt{\frac{2t}{\pi}}\left(|I_{2}|+|I_{3}|\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{\sqrt{\pi}}\frac{e^{-2t}}{\sqrt{2t}}+\sqrt{\frac{2t}{\pi}}\left(\frac{M}{t}(1-e^{-t})+Ce^{-t}\right)\,.

The claim follows.
∎

4.
The measure of circles

We now restrict our attention to
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
. In this section, we show that the measure of a circle can computed as a limit via a sequence of Schwartz functions. For a measure
\mu
which is Fourier transformable, this will allow us to express the measure
\hbox{\hskip 451.29065pt\hskip-3.01274pt\hbox{$\mu$}\hskip-3.01274pt\hskip-451.29065pt\raisebox{15.30566pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-3.34055pt}{\resizebox{53617400}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14236pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.98pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14285pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.5pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}\hskip-451.29065pt\hskip 451.29065pt}(C_{r})
as a formula in terms of the original measure
\mu
.

We will more generally show that we can use any sequence of Schwartz functions that satisfy a condition, which we will call and an approximate
r
-circle. While approximate
r
-circles are easy to construct, for applications we will need such a sequence with nice, easily computable Fourier transforms. We will construct one such example in Section
5
.

We now introduce the notion of an approximate
r
-circle.

Definition 4.1
.

Let
r\geq 0
. We say that a sequence
\left\{f_{n}\right\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
in
{\mathcal{S}}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})
is an
approximate
r
-circle
if the following conditions are satisfied:

(C1)

\lim\limits_{n\rightarrow\infty}f_{n}(x)=1_{C_{r}}(x)
for all
x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
;

(C2)

there exists some
F\in{\mathcal{S}}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})
such that

|f_{n}(x)|\leq F(x)\qquad\forall x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\,,\forall n\in\mathbb{N}\,.

In the following lemma, we see that for any measure in
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{M}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})
, the measure of
C_{r}
can be computed as a limit via an approximate
r
-circle.

Lemma 4.2
.

Let
\mu
be a strongly tempered measure.
Then for any approximate
r
-circle
\left\{f_{n}\right\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
, we have

(4.1)

\mu(C_{r})=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}f_{n}(x)\,\mbox{d}\mu(x)\,.

Proof.

By Fact
2.4
, if
\mu
is strongly tempered then
f\in L^{1}(|\mu|)
for every
f\in{\mathcal{S}}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})
. In particular, if
\left\{f_{n}\right\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is an approximate
r
-circle and
F
is as in condition
(C2)
, then
F\in L^{1}(|\mu|)
. Then by condition
(C1)
and the dominated convergence theorem, we have

\mu(C_{r})=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}1_{C_{r}}(x)\,\mbox{d}\mu(x)=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}f_{n}(x)\,\mbox{d}\mu(x)=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}f_{n}(x)\,\mbox{d}\mu(x)\,.

∎

Remark 4.3
.

Using the monotone convergence theorem, we also get that (
4.1
) holds for any decreasing sequence
\left\{f_{n}\right\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of functions in
L^{1}(|\mu|)
satisfying
(C1)
.

As an immediate consequence of Lemma
4.2
we get the following result, which allows us calculate the intensity of a circle in the diffraction in terms of the autocorrelation measure.

Corollary 4.4
.

If
\mu\in\mathcal{S}\mathcal{M}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
and
\left\{f_{n}\right\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is an approximate
r
-circle, then

\hbox{\hskip 441.65944pt\hskip-3.01274pt\hbox{$\mu$}\hskip-3.01274pt\hskip-441.65944pt\raisebox{15.30566pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-3.34055pt}{\resizebox{52312104}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14236pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.98pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14285pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.5pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}\hskip-441.65944pt\hskip 441.65944pt}(C_{r})=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}f_{n}(x)\,\mbox{d}\,\hbox{\hskip 441.65944pt\hskip-3.01274pt\hbox{$\mu$}\hskip-3.01274pt\hskip-441.65944pt\raisebox{15.30566pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-3.34055pt}{\resizebox{52312104}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14236pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.98pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14285pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.5pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}\hskip-441.65944pt\hskip 441.65944pt}(x)=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}\hbox{\hskip 441.65944pt\hskip-5.7078pt\hbox{$f_{n}$}\hskip-5.7078pt\hskip-441.65944pt\raisebox{17.94456pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-3.34055pt}{\resizebox{52312104}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14236pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.98pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14285pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.5pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}\hskip-441.65944pt\hskip 441.65944pt}(x)\,\mbox{d}\mu(x)\,.\qed

This formula will become useful once we construct examples of approximate
r
-circles with functions that have nice Fourier transforms, which we do in the next section.

5.
Circles in diffraction

In this section, we construct an example of an approximate
r
-circle via the convolution of
\theta_{r}
with a family of Gaussian functions. We then use this approximate
r
-circle and Corollary
4.4
to derive an explicit formula for the diffraction measure of a circle in terms of the autocorrelation. A different example can be found in Section
A
.

Let
G_{n}
be the following family of two dimensional Gaussian functions

(5.1)

G_{n}(x)=e^{-n^{2}\|x\|^{2}}\qquad\forall x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\,,\ \forall n\in\mathbb{N}\,,

and for each
r>0
, let
\theta_{r}
be the uniform probability measure on the circle
C_{r}
. Consider the convolution
G_{n}*\theta_{r}
, defined by

G_{n}*\theta_{r}(x)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}G_{n}(x_{1}-r\cos\theta,x_{2}-r\sin\theta)\,\mbox{d}\theta\qquad\forall x=(x_{1},x_{2})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\,.

Since both
\theta_{r}
and
G_{n}
are rotationally invariant, their convolution
G_{n}*\theta_{r}
is also rotationally invariant.
With this observation, it is easy to show the following.

Lemma 5.1
.

For each
r>0
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we have

(5.2)

G_{n}*\theta_{r}(x)=I_{0}(2n^{2}r\|x\|)e^{-n^{2}(\|x\|^{2}+r^{2})}\qquad\forall x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\,.

Proof.

Using rotational invariance of
G_{n}*\theta_{r}
, for each
x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
we have:

\displaystyle G_{n}*\theta_{r}(x)=G_{n}*\theta_{r}(\|x\|,0)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}e^{-n^{2}((\|x\|-r\cos\theta)^{2}+r^{2}\sin^{2}\theta)}\,\mbox{d}\theta

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi}e^{-n^{2}(\|x\|^{2}+r^{2})}\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}e^{2n^{2}r\|x\|\cos\theta}\,\mbox{d}\theta=I_{0}(2n^{2}r\|x\|)e^{-n^{2}(\|x\|^{2}+r^{2})}\,.

∎

Throughout the rest of this section, we fix
r>0
and define the functions

g_{n}:=2nr\sqrt{\pi}G_{n}\,,\qquad f_{n}=g_{n}*\theta_{r}=2nr\sqrt{\pi}G_{n}*\theta_{r}\,.

We will prove that
f_{n}
is an approximate
r
-circle. Note that by (
5.2
) we have

f_{n}(x)=2nr\sqrt{\pi}I_{0}(2n^{2}r\|x\|)e^{-n^{2}(\|x\|^{2}+r^{2})}\qquad\forall x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\,.

First, we show that
f_{n}
converges pointwise to
1_{C_{r}}
.

Lemma 5.2
.

For each
r>0
, the sequence
\left\{f_{n}\right\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is an approximate
r
-circle.

Proof.

Let us first note that
f_{n}\in{\mathcal{S}}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})
for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
by Theorem
2.8
, since
G_{n}
is a Schwartz function and
\theta_{r}
is compactly supported.

Next, observe that

(5.3)

\begin{split}f_{n}(x)&=\frac{2nr\sqrt{\pi}I_{0}(2n^{2}r\|x\|)}{e^{n^{2}(\|x\|^{2}+r^{2})}}=\frac{2n\sqrt{r\pi\|x\|}I_{0}(2n^{2}r\|x\|)}{e^{2n^{2}r\|x\|}}\cdot\frac{\sqrt{r}e^{2n^{2}r\|x\|}}{\sqrt{\|x\|}e^{n^{2}(\|x\|^{2}+r^{2})}}\\
&=u(2n^{2}r\|x\|)\sqrt{\frac{r}{\|x\|}}e^{-n^{2}(\|x\|-r)^{2}}\end{split}

where

u(t):=\frac{\sqrt{2\pi t}I_{0}(t)}{e^{t}}\,.

We now can show that the two conditions (C1) and (C2) of Definition
4.1
hold.

(C1):

Since
I_{0}(0)=1
, when
x=0
we have

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}f_{n}(0)=2nr\sqrt{\pi}e^{-n^{2}r^{2}}=0\,.

Next, by Theorem
3.6
and (
5.3
), for all
x\neq 0
we have

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}f_{n}(x)=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\sqrt{\frac{r}{\|x\|}}e^{-n^{2}(\|x\|-r)^{2}}=\begin{cases}1&\|x\|=r\\
0&\|x\|\neq 0\ \text{and}\ \|x\|\neq r\,.\end{cases}\,.

This proves (C1).

(C2):
The function
u
is continuous on
[0,\infty)
and satisfies

\lim_{t\rightarrow\infty}u(t)=1\,.

This implies that there exists some constant
A
such that
|u(t)|\leq A
for all
t\in[0,\infty)
.
In particular,

|u(2n^{2}r\|x\|)|\leq A\qquad\forall n\in\mathbb{N},\forall x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\,.

By (
5.3
) we then have

|f_{n}(x)|\leq A\sqrt{\frac{r}{\|x\|}}e^{-n^{2}(\|x\|-r)^{2}}\leq A\sqrt{\frac{r}{\|x\|}}e^{-(\|x\|-r)^{2}}=:\psi_{1}(x)\,.

In particular, we get

(5.4)

|f_{n}(x)|\leq A\sqrt{2}e^{-(\|x\|-r)^{2}}\qquad\forall\|x\|\geq\frac{r}{2}\,.

Next, let
x\in B_{\frac{r}{2}}
. Then, for any
y\in C_{r}
we have

\|x-y\|\geq r-\|x\|\geq\frac{r}{2}\,.

From this, we see that

\displaystyle 0\leq g_{n}*\theta_{r}(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}g_{n}(x-y)\,\mbox{d}\theta_{r}(y)=\int_{C_{r}}g_{n}(x-y)\,\mbox{d}\theta_{r}(y)

\displaystyle=2rn\sqrt{\pi}\int_{C_{r}}e^{-n^{2}\|x-y\|^{2}}\,\mbox{d}\theta_{r}(y)

\displaystyle\leq 2rn\sqrt{\pi}\int_{C_{r}}e^{-n^{2}r^{2}/4}\,\mbox{d}\theta_{r}(y)

\displaystyle=2rn\sqrt{\pi}e^{-n^{2}r^{2}/4}\leq 2r\sqrt{\pi}\cdot c_{1}=:c_{2}\,,

where

c_{1}:=\sup\{ne^{-n^{2}r^{2}/4}:n\in\mathbb{N}\}<\infty\,.

Now, pick some
\psi_{2}\in C_{\mathsf{c}}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})
such that

\psi_{2}\geq c_{2}1_{B_{\frac{r}{2}}}\,.

Then, we have

(5.5)

|f_{n}(x)|\leq\psi_{2}(x)\qquad\forall\|x\|\leq\frac{r}{2}\,.

Therefore, combining (
5.4
) and (
5.5
) we get

|f_{n}(x)|\leq\psi_{1}(x)+\psi_{2}(x)\qquad\forall\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\,.

Setting
F:=\psi_{1}+\psi_{2}\in{\mathcal{S}}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})
, we showed condition (C2).
∎

We have thus constructed an explicit approximate
r
-circle. Since we can easily compute

f_{n}

\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt

, we can now construct an explicit formula for the diffraction measure
\hbox{\hskip 451.29065pt\hskip-2.58865pt\hbox{$\gamma$}\hskip-2.58865pt\hskip-451.29065pt\raisebox{15.30566pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-3.34055pt}{\resizebox{53617400}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14236pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.98pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14285pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.5pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}\hskip-451.29065pt\hskip 451.29065pt}(C_{r})
for a circle

of a given radius

, in terms of the autocorrelation
\gamma
. To derive the formula, we simply need to apply Corollary
4.4
for the approximate
r
-circle

, and compute

\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt

.

that

is an approximate
r
-circle, so by Corollary

, we have the formula

for any measure

. It is easy to see that

\hbox{\hskip 451.29065pt}(y)=\frac{\pi}{n^{2}}e^{-\pi^{2}\|y\|^{2}/n^{2}}\qquad\forall y\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\,.

Moreover, by Proposition
3.4
we have

\hbox{\hskip 451.29065pt\hskip-4.56134pt\hbox{$\theta_{r}$}\hskip-4.56134pt\hskip-451.29065pt\raisebox{17.94456pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-3.34055pt}{\resizebox{53617400}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14236pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.98pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14285pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.
